# Psychology — Questions

Answers: [answers.md](answers.md). Question numbers match answer numbers.

## VCE Units 1 & 2

### Unit 1 AOS 2: How does the brain function?

#### Approaches to understanding the brain

*Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments) · Prerequisites: none*

**Q1.** Roger Sperry's split-brain research involved participants whose corpus callosum had been surgically cut. When an object was flashed to the left visual field only, these participants typically could not
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-1 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. point to the object with their left hand
- B. verbally name the object
- C. see anything in the left visual field
- D. recognise the object by touch with the left hand

**Q2.** Which neuroimaging technique detects changes in blood oxygen levels to show which brain regions are active during a task, and has relatively good spatial resolution?
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-2 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Computed tomography (CT)
- B. Lesion analysis
- C. Electroencephalography (EEG)
- D. Functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI)

**Q3.** A researcher records electrical activity at the scalp while participants listen to tones, looking at changes over milliseconds. The main strength of this technique is its
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-3 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. high spatial resolution of deep brain structures
- B. ability to show the brain's anatomy in detail
- C. high temporal resolution
- D. use of a radioactive tracer

**Q4.** Phineas Gage survived an accident in which an iron rod passed through his frontal lobe. Reports of personality change after the accident are best described as
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-4 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. a longitudinal study using fMRI
- B. an experiment with a control group
- C. a case study that suggested a link between the frontal lobe and personality
- D. a double-blind trial of a brain stimulation technique

**Q5.** Which of the following is a limitation of using case studies of brain damage to understand brain function?
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-5 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. They can never be ethically conducted
- B. Findings may not generalise because the damage is unique to each individual
- C. They always use large random samples
- D. They cannot provide detailed information

**Q6.** Explain one similarity and one difference between fMRI and EEG as techniques used to study the brain. (3 marks)
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-6 -->
*Info: Conceptual · about 3 marks · about 4 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); short answer, as in school exams and SACs*

*Written answer*

<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-7 -->
*Info: Conceptual · about 3 marks · about 4 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); short answer, as in school exams and SACs*

*Written answer*

<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-8 -->
*Info: Evaluation · about 3 marks · about 4 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); short answer, as in school exams and SACs*

*Written answer*

**Q9.** Paul Broca's patient 'Tan' could understand speech well but could produce only the one word 'tan'. After the patient's death, post-mortem examination found damage to which region?
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-9 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the left frontal lobe
- B. the right temporal lobe
- C. the left occipital lobe
- D. the cerebellum

**Q10.** A limitation of lesion analysis in human patients with brain damage is that
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-10 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the damage cannot be seen on any scan
- B. the results always contradict those from animal studies
- C. researchers cannot control the location or extent of the damage
- D. participants cannot give informed consent

**Q11.** A patient arrives at an emergency department with a sudden severe headache and possible bleeding in the brain. Which technique is most appropriate for a quick check of the bleed?
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-11 -->
*Info: Evaluation · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. electroencephalography (EEG)
- B. functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI)
- C. positron emission tomography (PET)
- D. computed tomography (CT)

**Q12.** Positron emission tomography (PET) produces images by detecting
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-12 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. changes in blood oxygen levels in the brain
- B. the uptake of a radioactive tracer that indicates metabolic activity
- C. electrical signals from the scalp
- D. the absorption of X-rays passing through the head

**Q13.** Compared with CT, structural MRI has the advantage that it
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-13 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. gives more detailed soft-tissue images without ionising radiation
- B. is cheaper and faster to perform
- C. measures electrical activity in neurons
- D. uses a radioactive tracer to show blood flow

**Q14.** In fMRI studies, researchers often subtract the brain activity recorded during a control task from that recorded during an experimental task. The purpose of this is to
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-14 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. remove the need for a control group
- B. increase the temporal resolution of the scan
- C. isolate activity linked to the cognitive process of interest
- D. measure electrical activity that the BOLD signal cannot show

**Q15.** A split-brain participant holds a familiar object in their right hand, out of sight, and can usually say what it is. This is because
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-15 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the corpus callosum carries touch information from the right hand to the right hemisphere
- B. touch is processed equally by both hemispheres
- C. the right hand is controlled by the right hemisphere, which can name objects
- D. touch from the right hand is processed by the left hemisphere, which controls speech

**Q16.** Lateralisation of brain function refers to the idea that
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-16 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. each hemisphere performs identical functions
- B. some functions are mainly controlled by one hemisphere rather than the other
- C. functions are spread evenly across the whole cortex
- D. the lateral (outer) areas of the brain control all sensory input

**Q17.** A researcher lesions the hippocampus of some rats and leaves others intact, then compares maze performance between the two groups. This is best described as
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-17 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. a correlational study, because brain structure and behaviour are measured
- B. a case study, because individual rats are studied in depth
- C. an experiment, because the researcher manipulates the lesion (the independent variable)
- D. a longitudinal study, because the rats are observed over time

**Q18.** Before an MRI scan, people with metal implants (such as some surgical devices) are screened because the strong magnetic field can
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-18 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. absorb X-rays and hide the brain's structure
- B. move or heat metal objects in the body
- C. block the radio waves needed to make an image
- D. expose the person to ionising radiation

**Q19.** The electrical signals recorded by EEG mainly reflect
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-19 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

**Q20.** A key limitation of animal lesion studies is that
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-20 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. results may not generalise to humans because of species differences in brain structure and function
- B. the lesions can never be precisely placed

**Q21.** Which of the following is an example of a naturally occurring lesion used in case study research?
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-21 -->

**Q22.** The BOLD signal measured in fMRI is best described as
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-22 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- B. an indirect measure of neural activity, based on changes in oxygenated blood flow

- C. scanning is expensive and time-consuming, which limits the number of participants
- D. participants cannot be scanned more than once

**Q35.** A researcher chooses EEG rather than fMRI to study brain activity in children in a school classroom, because the equipment is portable and does not require a scanner. Which limitation of EEG must the researcher accept?

- C. It requires the injection of a radioactive tracer

**Q36.** A researcher proposes to create small lesions in the memory areas of healthy adult volunteers to see how their memory changes. Which is the most important reason an ethics committee would reject this proposal?
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-36 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. The sample of volunteers would be too small to generalise
- B. Lesions cannot be seen on any scan
- C. The lesions would cause permanent, unnecessary harm to people who gain no benefit from the study
- D. Memory cannot be measured accurately in adults

**Q37.** Researchers report that an fMRI finding about a memory task came from only 12 participants. Why would other researchers want the study repeated with a larger, independent sample?
<!-- id: psych12a1-appr-37 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. To check whether the finding is reliable and can be reproduced, rather than a chance result from a small sample
- B. To prove that the memory task caused the brain activity
- C. To allow the use of a radioactive tracer in the next study
- D. Because larger samples always show a larger brain

#### Neuroplasticity and adolescent brain development

*Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on) · Prerequisites: Structure and function of the brain*

**Q81.** Synaptic pruning during adolescence refers to
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-1 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the elimination of weaker, rarely used synaptic connections
- B. the production of myelin around all axons
- C. the strengthening of connections through repeated use
- D. the growth of new neurons in the hippocampus

**Q82.** Long-term potentiation (LTP) is best described as
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-2 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the formation of the myelin sheath
- B. a long-lasting strengthening of synaptic connections after repeated stimulation
- C. the loss of neurons after injury
- D. a weakening of synapses due to lack of stimulation

**Q83.** Compared with adults, the adolescent brain typically shows
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-3 -->

- A. a prefrontal cortex that is still developing, with the limbic system relatively more active
- B. no neuroplasticity
- C. no synaptic pruning
- D. a fully developed prefrontal cortex

**Q84.** A woman loses function in her left arm after a stroke damages part of her motor cortex. Over months of rehabilitation, a nearby undamaged region begins to control the arm. This is an example of
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-4 -->

- A. adaptive plasticity involving rerouting
- B. a reflex response
- C. synaptic pruning
- D. long-term depression

**Q91.** In a study of licensed London taxi drivers, researchers found a larger posterior hippocampus than in matched non-drivers. The most appropriate conclusion is that
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-11 -->

- B. the hippocampus controls the hand movements used when driving
- C. long-term, intensive spatial navigation is associated with structural change in the hippocampus

**Q92.** People who have been blind since early childhood sometimes show activity in the visual cortex when reading Braille by touch. This best illustrates
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-12 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. a fixed, innate function of the visual cortex that cannot change
- B. a reflex response in which touch automatically triggers vision
- C. the loss of myelin in the somatosensory cortex
- D. cross-modal reorganisation in response to altered sensory experience

**Q93.** The principle 'use it or lose it' in neuroplasticity means that
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-13 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. neural connections that are rarely activated are more likely to be weakened or eliminated
- B. skills, once learned, can never be forgotten or changed
- C. neurons that are not used daily die within a year
- D. the brain stops responding to experience after adolescence

<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-14 -->

- A. brain structure is set by genes and cannot be altered by the environment
- B. experience can change the physical structure of the brain
- C. dendritic branching decreases when animals are more active
- D. enriched environments remove myelin from axons

<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-15 -->

- A. neurogenesis and long-term potentiation

- C. synaptic pruning and myelination
- D. long-term potentiation and the formation of new neurons

- A. the time after which the brain can no longer form new synapses
- B. the age at which the brain reaches its maximum weight
- C. a stage when all neurons stop dividing and the brain is fully wired

**Q97.** Children often recover more function than adults after a similar brain injury. The main reason is that children's brains
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-17 -->

<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-18 -->

- B. maladaptive plasticity, in which reorganisation is linked to persistent pain
- C. long-term potentiation of a spinal reflex arc

<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-19 -->

- B. all neurons in a network firing at the same time during learning
- C. the strengthening of connections between neurons that are repeatedly active together

**Q100.** Synapse density in the prefrontal cortex is highest in early childhood and declines during adolescence. This pattern is most consistent with
<!-- id: psych12a1-neur-20 -->

- B. synaptic pruning of rarely used connections
- C. a general strengthening of every synapse in the cortex

### Unit 1 AOS 1: What influences psychological development?

#### Nature, nurture and the biopsychosocial approach

*Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on) · Prerequisites: none*

**Q161.** A psychologist argues that a child's shyness is largely inherited from her parents. This view emphasises
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-1 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. nature
- B. the social factor of the biopsychosocial approach
- C. cultural learning
- D. nurture

**Q162.** The biopsychosocial approach to understanding behaviour proposes that
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-2 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. social factors are unimportant for mental health
- B. behaviour is caused only by biological factors
- C. behaviour results from the interaction of biological, psychological and social factors
- D. psychological factors outweigh biological factors

**Q163.** Which of the following is a psychological factor in the biopsychosocial approach?
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-3 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Neurotransmitter levels
- B. Peer pressure
- C. Pattern of negative thinking
- D. Genetic predisposition

**Q164.** Identical twins raised apart are found to have very similar personality traits. This finding is most likely used to support the influence of
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-4 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. culture
- B. schooling
- C. genetics
- D. parenting style

**Q165.** A student who has a family history of anxiety (genetic predisposition) develops an anxiety disorder after a stressful family move and an unsupportive school environment. This best demonstrates that
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-5 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. nature alone causes mental disorder
- B. nurture alone causes mental disorder
- C. biological, psychological and social factors can interact
- D. social factors are irrelevant

<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-9 -->

**Q170.** A toddler begins walking at about 12 months, around the same age as most toddlers, even though no one has taught her to walk. This best illustrates
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-10 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. learning through reinforcement from parents
- B. maturation, a genetically programmed sequence of physical development
- C. social modelling from older siblings
- D. cultural expectations about when children should walk

**Q171.** A child learns to show respect to elders by taking part in daily family routines in which older relatives are greeted first. This is an example of
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-11 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. genetic inheritance
- B. maturation
- C. socialisation through cultural learning, a form of nurture
- D. a neurotransmitter imbalance

**Q172.** In twin studies, the concordance rate is the likelihood that both twins share a trait. Concordance for a trait is higher in identical twins than in non-identical twins. This pattern most strongly suggests
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-12 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the trait is determined entirely by the shared environment
- B. the trait has no genetic component
- C. environmental influences are stronger than genetic influences for every trait
- D. a genetic influence on the trait

**Q173.** Which pairing correctly classifies each factor as biological, psychological or social?
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-13 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. family conflict: social; low self-esteem: psychological; hormone levels: biological
- B. family conflict: biological; low self-esteem: social; hormone levels: psychological
- C. family conflict: psychological; low self-esteem: biological; hormone levels: social
- D. family conflict: social; low self-esteem: biological; hormone levels: psychological

**Q174.** Which of the following is an example of a nurture influence on a child's development?
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-14 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. inheriting the same blood type as a parent
- B. being taught to use a knife and fork by a parent at mealtimes
- C. a grasp reflex that is present from birth
- D. a voice that deepens during puberty

**Q175.** A man loses his job and begins to feel persistently low. Which explanation best reflects the biopsychosocial approach?
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-15 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. biological causes only, such as a change in brain chemistry
- B. social causes only, namely the loss of income
- C. several interacting factors, such as disrupted sleep, self-critical thinking and reduced contact with friends
- D. psychological causes only, because biology plays no role in mood

**Q176.** Which statement is the best example of reductionism in psychology?
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-16 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. considering how thoughts, emotions and family relationships together affect a person's mood
- B. using a model that includes biological, psychological and social levels of explanation
- C. recognising that a person's behaviour is shaped by many interacting influences
- D. explaining all anxiety disorders purely in terms of genes

**Q177.** Children who are not exposed to spoken language during early childhood often have lasting difficulty acquiring language later. This best supports the idea of
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-17 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. a critical period in which language exposure is needed for normal development
- B. language being fully determined by genes present at birth
- C. maturation alone, with no need for environmental input
- D. language skills being unaffected by early experience

**Q178.** Non-identical (dizygotic) twins share approximately what proportion of their genes?
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-18 -->

- A. 25%
- B. 50%
- C. 75%
- D. 100%

**Q179.** Which statement about nature and nurture is most accurate?
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-19 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. They operate separately, so each can be measured without reference to the other
- B. They are two independent causes that never influence each other
- C. Development results from the ongoing interaction of inherited and environmental factors
- D. Nature determines development before birth, after which nurture has no further influence

**Q180.** A health worker assesses a patient with chronic pain. Which approach best reflects the biopsychosocial model?
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-20 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. prescribing medication only, because pain is a physical problem
- B. referring the patient for counselling only, because pain is a mental problem
- C. checking only physical symptoms, since social factors have no bearing on pain
- D. assessing physical symptoms, stress and coping, and family and work circumstances together

<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-21 -->

- B. psychological factors

<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-22 -->

- B. observational learning, a form of nurture
- C. an inherited temperament that causes fear of animals

**Q183.** Which of the following is an environmental influence that acts before birth?

**Q184.** Which of the following best describes the nature-nurture debate?
<!-- id: psych12a1-natu-24 -->

- A. a long-running question about how much inherited and environmental factors contribute to development
- B. a debate that was settled in favour of genes in the twentieth century
- C. a question about whether psychological development occurs only after birth

**Q185.** A child has a genetic tendency towards musical ability. She develops this ability only after many hours of lessons and practice. Which principle does this best illustrate?

#### Emotional development and attachment

*Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments) · Prerequisites: Nature, nurture and the biopsychosocial approach*

**Q201.** In Ainsworth's Strange Situation, an infant who shows little distress when the caregiver leaves and ignores the caregiver on return would be classified as
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-1 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. insecure-avoidant
- B. disorganised only
- C. securely attached
- D. insecure-resistant

**Q202.** In the Strange Situation, a securely attached infant typically
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-2 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. shows no interest in exploring the room
- B. uses the caregiver as a secure base, is distressed when they leave and is easily comforted on return
- C. is indifferent to the stranger and the caregiver
- D. resists contact with the caregiver on return

**Q203.** Harlow's experiments with infant rhesus monkeys and two surrogate mothers (wire with milk, cloth without milk) found that the infants
<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-3 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. showed no preference
- B. were unaffected by being separated from their real mothers
- C. preferred the wire mother because she provided food
- D. spent more time clinging to the cloth mother, suggesting contact comfort is important for attachment

**Q204.** Which is an ethical concern commonly raised about Harlow's research?

<!-- id: psych12a1-emot-11 -->

<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-2 -->

<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-4 -->

**Q277.** A 5-year-old believes that the wind is made by people blowing air through giant machines. Piaget would describe this belief as
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-37 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. animism, because the wind is treated as alive
- B. artificialism, because natural events are believed to be made by people
- C. egocentrism, because the child cannot see the view of others
- D. conservation, because the child understands that the volume of air is constant

**Q278.** A 2-year-old has only used a spoon to scoop soft food. When given a bowl of hard, sticky porridge, she changes the way she holds the spoon and uses a different movement to scrape the bowl. Piaget would describe this change as
<!-- id: psych12a1-cogn-38 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. assimilation, because she fits the new bowl into her existing spoon schema
- B. centration, because she focuses on only one feature of the bowl
- C. reversibility, because she can undo the movement she made
- D. accommodation, because she modifies her existing schema to fit the new situation

**Q280.** A child watches juice poured from a short wide glass into a tall thin glass, then says the tall glass has more. She is most likely in Piaget's:
<!-- id: vce-psych12-pg-c1 -->
*Challenge question*
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams; harder than most exam questions*

- A. Concrete operational stage
- B. Sensorimotor stage, because she lacks object permanence
- C. Pre-operational stage, because she cannot yet conserve
- D. Formal operational stage

#### Typical and atypical behaviour

*Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments) · Prerequisites: none*

**Q321.** Which of the following is the best definition of atypical behaviour?
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-1 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Behaviour that is always a sign of mental illness
- B. Behaviour that is statistically uncommon or differs from what is expected for a person's age, culture and context
- C. Behaviour that is illegal
- D. Behaviour that other people dislike

**Q322.** Behaviour that helps a person cope with demands and function effectively in their environment is described as
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-2 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. atypical
- B. dysfunctional
- C. maladaptive
- D. adaptive

**Q323.** Speaking loudly to a group in a lecture hall is typical, but speaking at the same volume in a library is not. This demonstrates the importance of
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-3 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. context in judging behaviour
- B. genetics in determining behaviour
- C. age in determining behaviour
- D. intelligence in determining behaviour

**Q324.** On the mental wellbeing continuum, a person who is functioning well with only occasional mild stress would be placed
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-4 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. outside the continuum
- B. at the mental health disorder end
- C. in the middle of the continuum as a mental health problem
- D. towards the mental wellbeing (flourishing) end

**Q325.** A behaviour is most likely to be considered maladaptive if it
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-5 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. is unusual in a person's culture
- B. causes the person distress or interferes with daily functioning
- C. is chosen by the person
- D. differs from what most people do

**Q328.** Describe two criteria that psychologists use to decide whether a person's behaviour is a cause for concern. (2 marks)
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-8 -->

*Written answer*

**Q329.** Which of the following best describes the criterion of statistical infrequency?
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-9 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. A behaviour is judged atypical when only a small proportion of people in a comparable group show it
- B. A behaviour is judged atypical when most people in a comparable group show it
- C. A behaviour is judged atypical when it causes the person distress
- D. A behaviour is judged atypical when a law prohibits it

**Q330.** About 10 per cent of people are left-handed, so left-handedness is statistically uncommon. Which statement is best supported by this example?
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-10 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Left-handedness is a symptom of an underlying mental disorder
- B. Statistical rarity does not, on its own, mean a behaviour is harmful or maladaptive
- C. Left-handedness is typical in every culture because it is biologically determined
- D. Only behaviours shown by fewer than 1 per cent of people can be considered atypical

**Q331.** A four-year-old has a tantrum when asked to share toys. Why is this behaviour best judged against age-based expectations?
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-11 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Because tantrums are always a sign of a disorder in any child
- B. Because the behaviour is atypical at any age, whatever the child's developmental stage
- C. Because the behaviour is common for young children at this stage, so age helps determine what is typical
- D. Because the child's intelligence determines whether the behaviour is typical

**Q332.** A person feels distressed after a poor night's sleep but still works, studies and keeps up with friends as usual. Which conclusion is best supported?
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-12 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. The person has a mental disorder, because any distress is a symptom of disorder
- B. The person is displaying maladaptive behaviour, because distress always means functioning is impaired
- C. The person is displaying behaviour that is atypical for an adult, so it is a clear cause for concern
- D. The person is showing a temporary, typical response that does not stop them functioning

**Q333.** Which feature would most strongly suggest that a person's sadness after the death of a family member has moved beyond a typical grief response?
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-13 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. The sadness is severe, has lasted for many months and interferes with daily functioning
- B. The person cries at the funeral
- C. The person prefers to spend a few weeks alone
- D. The person feels sad on the anniversary of the death

**Q334.** Direct eye contact is usual in many Australian conversations, but in some other cultures prolonged direct eye contact with an elder is considered disrespectful. What does this example best illustrate?
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-14 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Eye contact is a universal sign of confidence and honesty
- B. Whether behaviour is typical depends on the cultural norms of the group in which it occurs
- C. Cultural differences have little effect on how behaviour is judged
- D. The same behaviour always has the same meaning across societies

**Q335.** What is the key difference between a mental health problem and a mental disorder on the mental wellbeing continuum?
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-15 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. A mental health problem is always more serious than a mental disorder
- B. There is no meaningful difference, and the two terms are used interchangeably
- C. A mental disorder is a diagnosable condition, usually with persistent and significant effects on functioning, while a mental health problem may be less severe, shorter-lived or less disabling
- D. A mental disorder occurs only in people who have experienced a traumatic event

**Q336.** Which statement about typical and adaptive behaviour is correct?
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-16 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Behaviour that is atypical is always maladaptive
- B. Behaviour that is typical is always adaptive, because most people do it
- C. Behaviour can only be adaptive if it is typical for the person's culture
- D. Typical behaviour can be maladaptive, and atypical behaviour can be adaptive

**Q337.** Why do psychologists caution against labelling a person as disordered on the basis of a single atypical behaviour?
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-17 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. A single atypical behaviour does not show whether the distress, impaired functioning or persistence criteria are met
- B. Disorders can only be diagnosed in people over 65
- C. Atypical behaviour is never harmful, so it never needs attention
- D. Only a family member is permitted to judge a person's behaviour

**Q338.** Which example best illustrates atypical behaviour that is adaptive rather than harmful?
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-18 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. A person who avoids all social contact and feels distressed whenever others are near
- B. A person who shouts at colleagues and has lost several jobs
- C. A person who can memorise long strings of numbers accurately and uses this ability successfully in their work
- D. A person who checks the door lock repeatedly until their hands are sore

**Q339.** Which of the following is an example of a behaviour judged atypical because it violates a social norm, rather than because it is statistically uncommon?
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-19 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Having a rare blood type
- B. Having naturally red hair
- C. Having an exceptionally high IQ
- D. Wearing a swimsuit to a formal wedding ceremony

**Q340.** Which statement about social norms is correct?
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-20 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Social norms are shared expectations about how people should behave in a group, and they can differ between groups and change over time
- B. Social norms are fixed and identical in every society
- C. Social norms apply only to children and adolescents
- D. Social norms are the same as laws, so breaking one is always illegal

**Q341.** When judging whether a behaviour is a cause for concern, which combination of information is most useful?
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-21 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Only the person's own opinion of the behaviour
- B. How the behaviour compares with the person's age, culture and context, and whether it causes distress or impairs functioning
- C. How rare the behaviour is in the general population, regardless of its effects
- D. Whether the behaviour matches the observer's personal preferences

**Q342.** A teenager spends most evenings reading alone and has only a few close friends. However, the teenager is happy, does well at school and reports no distress. Which conclusion is best supported?
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-22 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. The behaviour is unusual, but it is not maladaptive or a cause for concern on the criteria given
- B. The behaviour is a mental disorder, because it is uncommon among teenagers
- C. The behaviour must be treated, because the teenager has few friends
- D. The behaviour is maladaptive, because it differs from what most teenagers do

**Q343.** A child is shy with new people but makes friends over time, enjoys school and copes with new routines. Which conclusion is best supported?
<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-23 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. The child is showing maladaptive behaviour because they avoid new people
- B. The child has a mental disorder, because shyness is atypical for children
- C. The child's behaviour falls within the typical range of temperament, with no clear sign of concern
- D. The child's behaviour is atypical and harmful to their development, so it needs treatment

<!-- id: psych12a1-typi-33 -->

**Q359.** Using only a statistical approach, a person with an IQ of 140 would be described as atypical. This highlights a limitation of the approach because:
<!-- id: vce-psych12-ta-c1 -->
*Challenge question*
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams; harder than most exam questions*

- A. Statistics cannot measure intelligence, so IQ scores are not valid data
- B. Being statistically rare is not the same as being maladaptive or a problem
- C. Most people have an IQ of 140, so the statistical approach is unreliable

#### Mental wellbeing and categorising mental disorders

*Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on) · Prerequisites: Typical and atypical behaviour*

**Q360.** Which statement best describes mental wellbeing?

- A. The absence of any diagnosable mental disorder
- B. A state of emotional, social and cognitive functioning in which a person can cope with normal stressors and contribute to their community
- C. A permanent personality trait that does not change across the lifespan
- D. The ability to avoid all negative emotions

**Q361.** On the mental health continuum, a person who reports frequent positive emotions, feels they contribute to society and has a sense of purpose would most likely be described as
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-2 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. having a dimensional deficit
- B. languishing
- C. flourishing
- D. experiencing a categorical disorder

**Q362.** A clinician diagnoses a client with generalised anxiety disorder because the client meets a set number of specified symptoms from a diagnostic manual. This is an example of
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-3 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. a categorical approach
- B. a biopsychosocial approach
- C. a cultural relativist approach
- D. a dimensional approach

**Q363.** A key criticism of the categorical approach to classifying mental disorders is that it
<!-- id: psych12a2-mw-4 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. relies on a continuum of severity only
- B. does not use any diagnostic criteria
- C. cannot be used by clinicians

**Q364.** Which of the following is a feature of the dimensional approach to mental disorders?

- A. Disorders are either present or absent
- B. Symptoms are rated on a continuum of severity

Her friend Jo reports feeling empty, isolated and unmotivated most days, though he has no diagnosis. Using the mental health continuum, describe where each person is likely to sit and explain why. ### Unit 2 AOS 1: Social cognition and social influence

#### Person perception and attributions

*Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments) · Prerequisites: none*

**Q400.** Person perception refers to
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-1 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. an enduring set of beliefs about a social group
- B. the tendency to avoid people who are different
- C. the process of forming impressions and making judgements about other people
- D. the unconscious urge to behave like a majority

**Q401.** A new teacher assumes a student who is neatly dressed and attractive is also intelligent and hard-working. This is an example of
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-2 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the halo effect
- B. the fundamental attribution error
- C. the self-serving bias
- D. confirmation bias

**Q402.** Sam is late to a meeting and his manager concludes he is lazy, without considering that there was a train breakdown. This is best described as
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-3 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the self-serving bias
- B. a stereotype threat
- C. a situational attribution
- D. the fundamental attribution error

**Q403.** After winning a trophy, a netballer says it was due to her hard training. After losing a final, she blames the umpiring. This pattern is called
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-4 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the self-serving bias
- B. the fundamental attribution error
- C. cognitive dissonance
- D. the halo effect

**Q404.** Which of the following is an example of an external (situational) attribution?
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-5 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. 'She failed the test because she is not clever.'
- B. 'He yelled at the waiter because he has a bad temper.'
- C. 'I was late because the road was closed for repairs.'
- D. 'They won because they are naturally talented.'

**Q405.** Describe the difference between a dispositional attribution and a situational attribution, using one example of each. (3 marks)
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-6 -->

*Written answer*

**Q406.** Priya sees a stranger snap at a shop assistant and concludes the stranger is an unpleasant person. Later, when Priya snaps at someone herself, she explains it was because she was exhausted. Identify the attribution bias illustrated and explain what it shows. (3 marks)
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-7 -->
*Info: Application · about 3 marks · about 4 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); short answer, as in school exams and SACs*

*Written answer*

**Q407.** Explain one way in which the halo effect could disadvantage a job applicant in an interview. (2 marks)
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-8 -->
*Info: Conceptual · about 2 marks · about 2.5 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); short answer, as in school exams and SACs*

*Written answer*

**Q408.** The primacy effect in person perception refers to
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-9 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the tendency for information presented first to carry more weight in forming an impression than later information
- B. the tendency for the most recent information to be remembered and weighted most heavily
- C. the tendency to judge people more favourably when they share our attitudes
- D. the belief that people generally get what they deserve in life

**Q409.** Which of the following is the best description of a schema in person perception?
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-10 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. a single, specific memory of meeting someone at a party
- B. a mental framework of organised knowledge about a type of person or situation that guides expectations
- C. a visible physical trait, such as height or hair colour, used to judge a person
- D. a sudden emotional reaction to a stranger's appearance

**Q410.** In Kelley's covariation model, which combination of information would most strongly lead an observer to make a situational attribution?
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-11 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. low consensus, low distinctiveness, high consistency
- B. high consensus, low distinctiveness, low consistency
- C. high consensus, high distinctiveness, high consistency
- D. low consensus, high distinctiveness, low consistency

**Q411.** Which of the following best describes a self-fulfilling prophecy?
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-12 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. a belief that a person's personality is fixed and cannot change over time
- B. the tendency to remember predictions that turned out correct and forget those that were wrong
- C. a prediction that is accurate only because the predictor has more information
- D. an expectation about someone that leads that person to behave in a way that confirms the expectation

**Q412.** Research comparing cultures suggests that the fundamental attribution error is
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-13 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. generally more pronounced in individualist cultures than in many collectivist cultures
- B. absent entirely in collectivist cultures, where people never explain behaviour through personal traits
- C. a universal bias that affects all cultures to exactly the same degree
- D. found only in people with a high level of formal education

**Q413.** The horns effect refers to
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-14 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the tendency to remember the first person met in a group
- B. a negative impression formed from one unfavourable trait, which then colours judgements of other, unrelated traits
- C. a positive impression formed from one favourable trait, which then colours judgements of other, unrelated traits
- D. the belief that a person's appearance reveals their hidden character

**Q414.** Which of the following best illustrates the just world hypothesis?
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-15 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. A manager promotes the candidate who scored highest in the selection tests.
- B. A driver who cut someone off is assumed to be careless, rather than rushed by an emergency.
- C. A colleague says a worker who was dismissed must have deserved it, because bad things only happen to people who do something wrong.
- D. A team member credits a win to their own skill but blames a loss on the referee.

**Q415.** Stereotype threat refers to
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-16 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. a fear of being physically harmed by members of an outgroup
- B. the tendency to agree with a majority opinion in order to avoid conflict
- C. a belief that one's own group is superior to all others
- D. a concern that one's performance will confirm a negative stereotype about one's group, which can itself reduce performance

**Q416.** After deciding her new neighbour is unfriendly, Mia notices every brief, abrupt moment and overlooks the friendly waves they give her. This best illustrates
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-17 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. confirmation bias, the tendency to seek out and interpret information in ways that support an existing belief
- B. the primacy effect, the tendency for early information to be remembered best
- C. the halo effect, where one positive trait shapes judgements about other traits
- D. cognitive dissonance, the discomfort created by holding conflicting beliefs or behaviours

**Q417.** The ultimate attribution error describes the tendency to
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-18 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. rate every member of one's own group as more similar to one another than members of other groups
- B. explain negative behaviour of outgroup members in terms of their character, but explain the same behaviour by ingroup members as the result of circumstances
- C. assume that the behaviour of others is always caused by their situation rather than their personality
- D. remember more information about outgroup members than about ingroup members

**Q418.** Which of the following is an example of a non-verbal cue that can shape a first impression?
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-19 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the content of a job applicant's résumé
- B. the length of time a person has worked in their previous job
- C. the tone of voice and eye contact an applicant uses during an interview
- D. the wording of references written by former employers

**Q419.** A person judges how common a trait is among members of a group by how easily examples come to mind. This best illustrates
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-20 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the availability heuristic
- B. the representativeness heuristic
- C. the anchoring effect
- D. the gambler's fallacy

**Q420.** Which of the following statements about first impressions is most accurate?
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-21 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. First impressions form only after a long period of interaction, once a person has enough evidence.
- B. First impressions form within seconds and can be difficult to change, even when new information contradicts them.
- C. First impressions are always accurate because they are based on objective evidence.
- D. First impressions are formed only from what a person says, not from how they look or behave.

**Q421.** Impression management refers to
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-22 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the tendency to form impressions of others from first meetings
- B. a bias in which people overestimate how much others notice their appearance and behaviour
- C. the deliberate effort to control the image that others form of us
- D. the process of changing one's own beliefs to match those of a group

**Q422.** Which statement correctly distinguishes a stereotype from prejudice?
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-23 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. A stereotype is a negative feeling towards a group; prejudice is a belief about the characteristics of that group.
- B. Stereotypes and prejudice are the same thing, and both always involve overt discrimination.
- C. A stereotype is a feeling of dislike; prejudice is the action of treating a group unfairly.
- D. A stereotype is a belief about the characteristics of a group; prejudice is a negative attitude or feeling towards members of that group.

**Q423.** The tendency to see members of an outgroup as more alike than members of one's own group is called
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-24 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the outgroup homogeneity effect
- B. the false consensus effect
- C. the ingroup favouritism effect
- D. the spotlight effect

**Q424.** The belief that certain personality traits tend to occur together, so that knowing one trait allows an observer to predict others, is known as
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-25 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the spotlight effect
- B. implicit personality theory
- C. the actor-observer bias
- D. cognitive dissonance

**Q425.** Which of the following is an example of the representativeness heuristic?
<!-- id: psych12a2-pp-26 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. remembering a dramatic plane crash and overestimating the risk of air travel
- B. accepting the first price offered as a reference point for negotiations
- C. assuming a quiet, bookish person is more likely to be a librarian than a salesperson, because they match the librarian prototype
- D. believing a coin is due to land tails after five heads in a row

#### Attitudes and cognitive dissonance

*Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments) · Prerequisites: Person perception and attributions*

**Q439.** In the tri-component model of attitudes, the 'affective' component is
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-1 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. a person's beliefs about an object
- B. a person's feelings about an object
- C. a person's behaviour towards an object
- D. a person's habits of thought

**Q440.** Cognitive dissonance is best described as
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-2 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the tendency to conform to group norms
- B. the tendency to attribute success to internal factors
- C. a pleasant feeling when attitudes and behaviours match
- D. an uncomfortable mental tension that arises when a person holds inconsistent cognitions or when behaviour conflicts with attitudes

**Q441.** A smoker who believes smoking is harmful keeps smoking and tells himself 'the risk is overstated and I'll quit later'. He is reducing dissonance by
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-3 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. changing his behaviour
- B. changing or minimising the importance of his attitude or belief
- C. obeying an authority figure
- D. applying the halo effect

**Q442.** Which of the following strategies would reduce cognitive dissonance by changing behaviour?
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-4 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Deciding the environment doesn't really matter
- B. Ignoring information that conflicts with the attitude
- C. A person who values the environment starts cycling instead of driving
- D. Adding a new belief that driving is necessary

**Q443.** Researchers find that people who are paid a small amount to tell others a boring task was interesting later rate the task as more enjoyable than those paid a large amount. This result is explained by
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-5 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. greater cognitive dissonance in the small payment group, resolved by changing their attitude
- B. greater conformity in the large payment group
- C. the self-serving bias
- D. greater external justification in the small payment group

**Q444.** Describe the three components of the tri-component model of attitudes, using an example about recycling. (3 marks)
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-6 -->
*Info: Conceptual · about 3 marks · about 4 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); short answer, as in school exams and SACs*

*Written answer*

**Q445.** Leila values honesty but tells a friend she loves a gift she actually dislikes. Using cognitive dissonance, explain what Leila is likely to experience and outline one way she might reduce it. (3 marks)

**Q446.** Explain why attitudes do not always predict behaviour. (2 marks)

**Q447.** Which of the following is an example of the behavioural component of an attitude?
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-9 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Signing a petition to ban single-use plastic bags
- B. Believing that single-use plastic bags harm marine life
- C. Feeling angry whenever a plastic bag is littered
- D. Thinking reusable bags are more convenient to carry

**Q448.** Which of the following makes an attitude most likely to predict a person's behaviour?
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-10 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. The attitude is held weakly and the person rarely thinks about it
- B. The attitude is strong, based on direct personal experience and often thought about
- C. The attitude is held only because the person's friends share it
- D. The attitude is about a topic the person has never encountered

**Q449.** After years of hearing her older brother describe a certain sport as boring and pointless, Mia now dislikes the sport, even though she has never played it. Her attitude is most likely formed through
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-11 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. operant conditioning, because she is rewarded each time she agrees with her brother
- B. cognitive dissonance, because her beliefs about sport conflict with each other
- C. observational learning from a trusted model
- D. the mere exposure effect, because she has heard about the sport often

**Q450.** Which statement best defines an attitude?
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-12 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. A temporary mood caused by the immediate environment
- B. A fixed personality trait that is present from birth
- C. A behaviour a person performs automatically without conscious thought
- D. A learned and relatively enduring evaluation of a person, object or issue

**Q451.** Which situation is most likely to produce cognitive dissonance?
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-13 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. A person who believes regular exercise is important skips every training session and then feels guilty
- B. A person who enjoys swimming goes to the pool on a hot day
- C. A person who likes pizza orders pizza for dinner
- D. A person who thinks the sky is blue looks outside and sees a blue sky

**Q452.** Recruits who endure a severe, painful initiation to join a club often come to rate the club more highly than recruits who had an easy entry. This is best explained by
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-14 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. conformity, because the recruits copy the views of existing members
- B. dissonance reduction, because the recruits increase the value they place on the club to justify their suffering
- C. the self-serving bias, because the recruits credit their entry to their own ability
- D. the halo effect, because the club's members seem attractive

**Q453.** After choosing between two equally attractive job offers, Rahul begins to rate the job he accepted as more appealing and the one he rejected as less appealing. This is best explained by
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-15 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the foot-in-the-door technique
- B. dissonance reduction after a decision, by changing how the options are evaluated
- C. the bystander effect
- D. the fundamental attribution error

**Q454.** Which factor is most likely to weaken the link between a person's attitude and their behaviour?
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-16 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. The behaviour is easy and inexpensive to perform
- B. The attitude is specific to the behaviour in question
- C. The person has had direct personal experience with the issue
- D. Strong social pressure from peers to act against the attitude

**Q455.** Which statement about attitudes and behaviour is correct?
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-17 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Attitudes always fully determine behaviour, so behaviour never affects attitudes
- B. Attitudes and behaviour are unrelated, so changing one never affects the other
- C. Behaviour can change attitudes only in childhood, after which attitudes are fixed
- D. Attitudes can influence behaviour, and behaviour can also change attitudes, so the influence can run in both directions

**Q456.** A child feels uneasy every time she hears the whirr of a dentist's drill. Later she feels uneasy at the sight of any dental clinic, even before anything painful happens. Her attitude towards dentists is best explained by
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-18 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. classical conditioning, because a neutral stimulus has been repeatedly paired with an unpleasant experience
- B. operant conditioning, because she is punished for going to the dentist
- C. observational learning from her parents
- D. cognitive dissonance, because her beliefs about dentists conflict with her feelings

**Q457.** A teenager receives praise from friends each time he speaks positively about a political party. Over time he begins to hold that view himself. This change is best explained by
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-19 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. classical conditioning, because the party's name is paired with a neutral sound
- B. operant conditioning, because positive reinforcement strengthens the expressed view
- C. cognitive dissonance, because he is uncomfortable with his views
- D. the mere exposure effect, because he hears the party's name often

**Q458.** Which statement reflects the affective component of an attitude towards a new school uniform policy?
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-20 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. The policy costs families more money than the previous one
- B. Uniforms have been part of this school's tradition for fifty years
- C. I feel frustrated every time I have to put on the uniform
- D. I wrote to the principal to ask for the policy to be reviewed

**Q459.** According to Festinger's theory, cognitive dissonance is strongest when
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-21 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. a person is pressured by an authority figure to do something they dislike
- B. two trivial beliefs that the person rarely thinks about are inconsistent
- C. a person is unaware of any inconsistency between their beliefs and actions
- D. a valued belief conflicts with an action the person freely chose to take

**Q460.** Which of the following is an example of an attitude formed through direct personal experience?
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-22 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Reading a news article about a flood in another state
- B. Hearing a friend describe a flood from years ago
- C. Living through a flood and losing property
- D. Watching a documentary about flooding

**Q461.** What is the main difference between a belief and an attitude?
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-23 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Beliefs are always true, whereas attitudes can be false
- B. There is no difference, as beliefs and attitudes are the same thing
- C. Attitudes include an evaluation of something as good or bad, whereas beliefs are information a person holds about it
- D. Beliefs include feelings of like or dislike, whereas attitudes are purely factual

**Q462.** Ravi believes that flying is much safer than driving, yet feels intense fear every time he boards a plane. Which statement best describes this situation?
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-24 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. The cognitive and affective components of his attitude are inconsistent
- B. His attitude is consistent because all of its components are positive
- C. His belief about safety is the affective component, while the fear is the behavioural component
- D. His attitude is consistent because he never acts on his fear

**Q463.** A new band's name appears on posters around campus many times. Without ever hearing their music, a student comes to like the band more. This is best explained by
<!-- id: psych12a2-ad-25 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the mere exposure effect
- B. cognitive dissonance, because the student's beliefs about the band are inconsistent
- C. the foot-in-the-door technique
- D. the self-serving bias, because the student credits their taste to their own judgement

**Q473.** A smoker knows smoking is harmful but keeps smoking, saying 'My grandfather smoked and lived to 90.' The smoker is:
<!-- id: vce-psych12-ac-c1 -->
*Challenge question*
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams; harder than most exam questions*

- A. Showing an attitude that has no affective component at all
- B. Reducing cognitive dissonance by changing a belief to fit the behaviour
- C. Making the fundamental attribution error about his grandfather
- D. Showing obedience to his grandfather as an authority figure

#### Stereotypes, prejudice and discrimination

*Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments) · Prerequisites: Attitudes and cognitive dissonance*

**Q474.** A stereotype is best defined as
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-1 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. a negative feeling directed at a group
- B. a generalised belief about the characteristics of members of a social group
- C. an unfair action taken against a group
- D. a belief formed through direct experience with every member of a group

**Q475.** A landlord refuses to rent a flat to a family because of their ethnic background. This behaviour is an example of
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-2 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. discrimination
- B. the halo effect
- C. a stereotype
- D. prejudice

**Q476.** Which statement correctly identifies the components of prejudice, stereotypes and discrimination?
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-3 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Stereotype: behavioural; prejudice: cognitive; discrimination: affective
- B. Stereotype: cognitive; prejudice: affective; discrimination: behavioural
- C. Stereotype: affective; prejudice: behavioural; discrimination: cognitive
- D. All three are cognitive

**Q477.** An employer who believes older workers cannot learn new technology reads a CV of a skilled 60-year-old and still hires a younger applicant. Which cognitive process most helps maintain the employer's stereotype?
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-4 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Actively adopting a dimensional approach
- B. Ignoring or discounting information that disconfirms it
- C. Seeking out disconfirming evidence
- D. Reducing dissonance by changing the belief

**Q478.** Which of the following would be most likely to reduce prejudice between two groups, according to contact research?
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-5 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Isolating the groups from each other
- B. Cooperative contact between groups working towards shared goals on an equal footing
- C. Highlighting differences between the groups
- D. Competition between groups for limited resources

**Q479.** Distinguish between prejudice and discrimination, using an example of each in a workplace. (3 marks)
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-6 -->

*Info: Conceptual · about 3 marks · about 4 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); short answer, as in school exams and SACs*
