**Q482.** Prejudice is best defined as
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-9 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. a generalised belief about the characteristics of a social group
- B. an unjustified, usually negative, attitude towards members of a group because of their group membership
- C. unfair treatment of people because of their group membership
- D. an accurate judgement about the typical behaviour of a group

**Q483.** Which term describes the tendency to evaluate and treat members of one's own group more favourably than members of other groups?
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-10 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. out-group homogeneity
- B. illusory correlation
- C. in-group favouritism
- D. fundamental attribution error

**Q484.** A student says, 'All the people from that suburb are the same; I can't tell any of them apart,' yet describes the members of their own school as individuals with different personalities. Which bias does the first statement most clearly illustrate?
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-11 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. out-group homogeneity effect
- B. in-group favouritism
- C. the halo effect
- D. self-serving bias

**Q485.** A teacher notices that two rare, unusual misbehaviours both occurred among students from a minority group, and concludes the two behaviours are linked to that group. Which cognitive error most likely explains this conclusion?
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-12 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. self-fulfilling prophecy
- B. just-world hypothesis
- C. fundamental attribution error
- D. illusory correlation

**Q486.** When a person meets a member of a stereotyped group who does not fit the stereotype, they may treat that person as a rare exception and keep the stereotype for the rest of the group. This process is known as
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-13 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. subtyping
- B. conformity
- C. scapegoating
- D. realistic group conflict

**Q487.** A teacher expects a student from a particular group to struggle with maths, so gives them easier tasks and less feedback. The student's results decline and the teacher concludes the expectation was right. This is an example of
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-14 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. fundamental attribution error
- B. in-group favouritism
- C. self-fulfilling prophecy
- D. realistic group conflict

**Q488.** A company requires all staff to attend full-time meetings during business hours. This excludes many people with caring responsibilities, most of whom are women, even though the company does not intend to disadvantage them. This is best described as
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-15 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. prejudice
- B. institutional discrimination
- C. a stereotype
- D. in-group favouritism

**Q489.** Which statement about stereotypes is correct?
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-16 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Stereotypes are always negative
- B. Stereotypes are feelings rather than beliefs
- C. Stereotypes can only be formed through direct experience with a group
- D. Stereotypes can be positive or negative, and may be accurate, partly accurate or inaccurate

**Q490.** In a laboratory study, participants are randomly placed into two groups using a meaningless criterion. When asked to allocate rewards between members of the two groups, they consistently give more to their own group. What does this finding best show?
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-17 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Prejudice requires a long history of conflict between groups
- B. Simply being categorised into a group can produce in-group favouritism
- C. Stereotypes are only formed through direct contact with group members
- D. Discrimination requires a negative attitude towards the other group

**Q491.** During an economic downturn, a community blames a small immigrant group for rising unemployment, even though there is no evidence the group caused it. This is an example of
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-18 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. scapegoating
- B. the contact hypothesis
- C. self-serving bias
- D. out-group homogeneity

**Q492.** Which statement best describes implicit prejudice?
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-19 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. An openly stated negative attitude that a person is willing to report
- B. An automatic, often unintentional negative association with a group that a person may not consciously endorse
- C. A stereotype that a person has deliberately chosen to adopt
- D. Discrimination that is carried out in secret

**Q493.** Which strategy most directly targets the cognitive component of prejudice?
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-20 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Enforcing laws that prohibit discriminatory hiring
- B. Providing education that challenges inaccurate stereotypes
- C. Separating groups to avoid conflict
- D. Repeating media images that show the group in typical roles

**Q494.** A woman feels anger whenever she hears about a particular group, but has never treated any member of that group unfairly. Which statement is correct?
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-21 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. She holds prejudice, the affective component, without having discriminated
- B. She shows discrimination because she feels anger
- C. She holds a stereotype, the cognitive component, without any prejudice
- D. She holds no prejudice because she has not acted on her feelings

**Q495.** In social identity theory, which process involves sorting people, including oneself, into groups such as 'us' and 'them'?
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-22 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. social categorisation
- B. social comparison
- C. social identification
- D. conformity

**Q496.** Which of the following is an example of discrimination rather than prejudice?
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-23 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. A manager privately believes women are less committed to their careers
- B. A manager feels uneasy whenever a female colleague joins a meeting
- C. A manager promotes a less qualified male colleague over a female colleague because of gender
- D. A manager describes people from one ethnic group as loud and disorganised

**Q497.** Which statement best describes the role of stereotypes in social cognition?
<!-- id: psych12a2-sp-24 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. They are behaviours that result directly from prejudice
- B. They always reflect accurate knowledge, so they should never be challenged
- C. They are only held by people who are prejudiced

**Q498.** Which change would most effectively reduce a self-fulfilling prophecy in a classroom where teachers expect some students to struggle?

- B. Grouping students by their expected ability
- C. Lowering expectations for all students

#### Heuristics and cognitive biases

*Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments) · Prerequisites: Person perception and attributions*

**Q511.** A heuristic is best described as
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-1 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. a type of long-term memory
- B. a systematic, step-by-step method that guarantees the correct answer
- C. a mental shortcut that allows quick judgements and decisions but may lead to errors
- D. an inherited reflex

**Q512.** After seeing news reports of several shark attacks, Tom decides swimming at the beach is very dangerous, even though attacks are rare. Which heuristic is Tom using?
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-2 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Availability heuristic
- B. Affect heuristic only
- C. Confirmation heuristic
- D. Representativeness heuristic

**Q513.** Ayesha assumes a quiet, bookish person at a party is more likely to be a librarian than a salesperson, ignoring that there are far more salespeople. This is the
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-3 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. self-serving bias
- B. availability heuristic
- C. representativeness heuristic
- D. confirmation bias

**Q514.** Which of the following best describes confirmation bias?
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-4 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Seeking, interpreting and remembering information that supports existing beliefs while overlooking contradictory evidence
- B. Overestimating one's own abilities
- C. Attributing one's failures to external causes
- D. Judging an event as likely because it is easy to recall

**Q515.** A voter reads only articles that support her preferred party and dismisses opposing articles as unreliable. This is an example of
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-5 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the representativeness heuristic
- B. the halo effect
- C. the hindsight bias
- D. confirmation bias

<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-9 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Availability heuristic
- B. Anchoring effect
- C. Hindsight bias
- D. Representativeness heuristic

**Q520.** Most people judge the sequence H T H T T H as more likely to come from a fair coin than the sequence H H H H H H, even though both sequences have the same probability. Which heuristic best explains this judgement?
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-10 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Affect heuristic
- B. Representativeness heuristic
- C. Recognition heuristic
- D. Anchoring and adjustment

**Q521.** Eli rejects a new medicine without reading the trial results because he dislikes the company that makes it. Which heuristic is he most likely using?
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-11 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Affect heuristic
- B. Availability heuristic
- C. Representativeness heuristic
- D. Recognition heuristic

**Q522.** After a team loses a grand final, a fan says, 'The signs were there all along; I knew they would lose.' Which bias does this statement show?

*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Belief perseverance
- B. Self-serving bias
- C. Hindsight bias
- D. Confirmation bias

**Q523.** Liam is sure that most students at his school prefer online lectures, because all his friends do. A survey later finds only 35% agree. Which bias is Liam showing?
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-13 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Belief perseverance
- B. Hindsight bias
- C. Optimism bias
- D. False consensus effect

**Q524.** A teacher is told that a student's gifted test results were fabricated, yet still believes the student is gifted because of those results. Which bias is involved?
<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-14 -->
*Info: Data analysis · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Belief perseverance
- B. Hindsight bias
- C. Framing effect
- D. Halo effect

**Q525.** Most drivers rate their own driving as better than average. Which bias best explains this?

- A. Gambler's fallacy

- A. Framing effect
- B. Availability heuristic

- D. Hindsight bias

**Q527.** A screening test correctly identifies a condition 95% of the time. The condition affects 1 in 100 people. Maya tests positive and concludes she has a 95% chance of having the condition, without considering how rare the condition is. Which error is she making?

- A. Base rate neglect
- B. Affect heuristic

<!-- id: psych12a2-hc-18 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Gambler's fallacy
- B. Illusion of control
- C. Hindsight bias
- D. Halo effect

- A. Illusion of control
- B. Hindsight bias
- C. Gambler's fallacy
- D. Affect heuristic

- A. Confirmation bias

- D. Halo effect

- A. Self-serving bias

**Q545.** After heavy news coverage of a plane crash, a person decides flying is more dangerous than driving. This reflects:
<!-- id: vce-psych12-hb-c1 -->
*Challenge question*
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams; harder than most exam questions*

- A. The anchoring bias: the first figure heard sets the estimate of risk
- B. The false-consensus bias: assuming others share the same fear of flying
- C. The halo effect: one bad feature makes the whole airline seem unsafe
- D. The availability heuristic: vivid, easily recalled examples seem more common

<!-- id: psych12a2-ob-15 -->

- A. obedience, because she is following the group's instructions

#### Conformity and group influence

*Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments) · Prerequisites: Obedience*

**Q578.** Conformity refers to
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-1 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. refusing to change one's opinion
- B. doing the opposite of what a group does
- C. obeying a direct order from an authority figure
- D. adjusting one's behaviour or beliefs to match those of a group or social norm

**Q579.** In Asch's line-judgement experiments, a participant tended to give an incorrect answer when
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-2 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. alone in a room
- B. all other group members, who were confederates, gave the same incorrect answer
- C. one confederate gave the correct answer
- D. the task was very difficult

**Q580.** A teenager starts wearing a style of shoes because everyone in the group does, even though she privately dislikes them. This is an example of
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-3 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. informational influence
- B. obedience
- C. internalisation
- D. normative influence leading to compliance

**Q581.** Which of the following would most likely reduce conformity in an Asch-type task?
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-4 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Having the participant respond publicly in front of the group
- B. Having at least one other person disagree with the majority
- C. Making the task more ambiguous
- D. Increasing the size of the majority to 15 people

**Q582.** Social loafing occurs when
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-5 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. a group makes riskier decisions than individuals
- B. individuals put in less effort when working in a group than when working alone
- C. individuals put in more effort when in a group
- D. people follow orders blindly

**Q586.** In Sherif's (1935) autokinetic experiments, a single point of light in a dark room appeared to move. When participants heard others' estimates, their own estimates converged towards a shared figure. This is an example of
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-9 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. normative influence, where people conform to be accepted by the group
- B. informational influence, where people look to others to judge an ambiguous situation
- C. obedience to an authority figure
- D. social loafing, where individuals reduce their effort in a group

**Q587.** A meta-analysis of Asch-type conformity studies carried out in many countries (Bond & Smith, 1996) found that conformity rates tended to be higher in
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-10 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. individualist cultures, which emphasise personal independence and self-expression
- B. urban populations, which have more anonymous contact with others
- C. collectivist cultures, which emphasise group harmony and belonging
- D. every culture equally, because conformity is entirely innate

**Q588.** In Latané, Williams and Harkins's (1979) study, participants made as much noise as they could by clapping or shouting, either alone or in groups. As group size increased, the noise produced by each person
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-11 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. increased, because people feel more energised in a crowd
- B. stayed the same, showing that group size makes no difference
- C. increased only when the participants could see one another
- D. decreased, showing social loafing

**Q589.** Group polarisation refers to the tendency for
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-12 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. groups to always reach a compromise between the most extreme positions held by members
- B. group discussion to strengthen members' initial shared views, so the group decision becomes more extreme than the average individual decision
- C. members to become less confident in their views after hearing others
- D. groups to split into two equal factions when they cannot agree

**Q590.** Which of the following is a symptom of groupthink, as described by Janis?
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-13 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. an illusion of invulnerability, in which members believe the group cannot make a serious mistake
- B. members deliberately seeking criticism from outside experts
- C. a leader inviting open disagreement before a decision is made
- D. decision makers repeatedly testing alternative options against evidence

**Q591.** Deindividuation is most likely to occur when people
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-14 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. are asked to state their own names and identify themselves to the group
- B. work on a task alone with a clear personal goal
- C. are anonymous in a large crowd and feel less personally accountable
- D. are paid individually for each contribution they make

**Q592.** In a variation of Asch's study, participants wrote their answers privately instead of stating them aloud in front of the group. Conformity in this condition was
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-15 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. higher, because participants felt more pressure to appear consistent
- B. lower than when answers were given publicly
- C. unchanged, because the group could still hear the other answers
- D. eliminated entirely, because private responses removed all social pressure

**Q593.** According to Moscovici's research on minority influence, a minority is most likely to change the majority's view when it
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-16 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. is made up of high-status members of the group
- B. gives different answers on every trial so that its position is unpredictable
- C. is consistent and confident in its position over time
- D. gives the same answer as the majority in public but a different answer in private

<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-17 -->

- A. 12
- B. 6

- A. 22%
- B. 25%

**Q596.** On a crowded train, a passenger collapses. Other passengers look around, see that no one is responding, and conclude that the situation is not serious. This is best explained by
<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-19 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-20 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. they had not been paying attention to the task at all

- A. a task that team members see as meaningful and important

<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-22 -->

<!-- id: psych12a2-cg-23 -->

#### Top-down and bottom-up processing

*Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on) · Prerequisites: Sensation and the visual system*

**Q653.** Bottom-up processing begins with
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-1 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the raw sensory input from a stimulus
- B. a decision about what to attend to
- C. stored memories only
- D. a person's expectations and prior knowledge

**Q654.** A person reading a sentence with a missing letter easily understands it because of the context of the surrounding words. This is mostly an example of
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-2 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. transduction
- B. bottom-up processing
- C. top-down processing
- D. sensory adaptation

**Q655.** A researcher shows participants an unfamiliar abstract pattern and asks them to describe exactly what they see, line by line. This task relies mostly on
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-3 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. bottom-up processing, because there is little prior knowledge to guide interpretation
- B. perceptual set
- C. confirmation bias
- D. top-down processing, because participants use prior expectations

**Q656.** Which factor is most likely to influence top-down processing?

- A. The number of photoreceptors in the retina
- B. The intensity of the stimulus
- C. The wavelength of the light
- D. The person's expectations, motivation and past experience

- A. different sensory receptors in the eye
- B. top-down processing influenced by expectation and emotion
- C. bottom-up processing only

**Q661.** A listener hears a recording in which a speech sound has been replaced by a cough, yet reports hearing the complete word as normal. This best illustrates
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-9 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. top-down processing filling in missing sensory information using knowledge of the word
- B. transduction converting the cough into a neural signal at the cochlea
- C. sensory adaptation making the cough seem quieter than the speech
- D. bottom-up processing in which the cough is registered as a speech sound

**Q662.** An experienced chess player glances at a mid-game board and quickly recognises a familiar opening pattern, while a novice sees only scattered pieces. The difference mainly reflects
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-10 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. stronger bottom-up processing in the novice, who registers every piece
- B. more efficient transduction of light in the expert's eyes
- C. top-down processing based on stored knowledge of chess patterns
- D. sensory adaptation to the board in the novice

**Q663.** The same shape is read as the letter B in the sequence A, B, C and as the number 13 in the sequence 12, 13, 14. Which statement best explains this?
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-11 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. The shape has different physical features in each sequence, so bottom-up processing gives different results
- B. Surrounding context leads to top-down processing that resolves the ambiguous shape differently in each sequence
- C. Transduction converts each sequence into a different set of neural signals
- D. Sensory adaptation to the first sequence makes the shape look different in the second

**Q664.** A listener with no knowledge of a language notices its individual sounds and pitch patterns before understanding any words. This is an example of
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-12 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. top-down processing, because the listener uses meaning to predict the next word
- B. perceptual set, because the listener expects the language to be unfamiliar
- C. bottom-up processing, because perception starts from the sensory features of the sounds
- D. sensory adaptation, because the listener becomes used to the sounds over time

**Q665.** Which statement about top-down processing is accurate?
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-13 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. It draws on stored knowledge, expectations and context to interpret sensory input
- B. It begins in the cortex and sends signals down to the eye's receptors
- C. It operates only when a stimulus is unclear, and not when it is clear
- D. It is the process by which light energy is converted into neural signals

**Q666.** Two groups drink the same wine. One group is told it is expensive and rates it as tastier than the other group, who is told nothing about its price. The difference is best attributed to
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-14 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. bottom-up processing, because the wine's chemical features differed between the groups
- B. transduction in the taste buds being more sensitive in the first group
- C. sensory adaptation, because the expensive label was noticed before the taste
- D. top-down processing, because the price expectation shaped how the taste was interpreted

**Q667.** A writer proofreads their own essay and misses a repeated word, as in 'the the'. Which explanation best accounts for this?
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-15 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Bottom-up processing registers each letter so precisely that repeats are always noticed
- B. Top-down expectation of a grammatical sentence makes the second word seem to belong, so the repeat goes unnoticed
- C. Transduction fails for common words, so the repeat is not sent to the brain
- D. Sensory adaptation makes the second instance of the word invisible

**Q668.** A radiologist told that a patient has a particular condition looks at an X-ray and reports a shadow that a colleague with no such information did not notice. Which statement best describes this?
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-16 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1.5 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Top-down processing is shaping how the same sensory input is interpreted, which can make expected features more likely to be seen
- B. Bottom-up processing has been reduced because the radiologist is looking for a specific condition
- C. The X-ray has been converted into different neural signals for an informed observer
- D. Sensory adaptation has made the shadow stand out more to the radiologist

**Q669.** A computer program can identify letters from their edges and strokes, but it has trouble with a smudged word that a person reads easily from the surrounding sentence. This contrast best illustrates
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-17 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the superiority of bottom-up feature analysis over top-down processing
- B. top-down processing being confined to the cortex of the brain
- C. the value of combining bottom-up feature analysis with top-down use of context
- D. transduction being the cause of the program's errors

**Q670.** Which condition is least likely to increase the influence of top-down processing on a perceptual judgement?
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-18 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. The stimulus being presented clearly and for a long time
- B. A strong expectation about what will be shown
- C. Context that makes one interpretation more likely
- D. A high level of anxiety about what might be seen

**Q671.** Participants shown the word 'doctor' are faster to recognise the word 'nurse' than participants who saw an unrelated word first. Which type of processing is most directly involved?
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-19 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. bottom-up processing, because the word 'nurse' is a physical stimulus
- B. transduction of the printed word into neural signals
- C. sensory adaptation to repeated letters in the words
- D. top-down processing, because prior exposure creates an expectation that speeds recognition

**Q672.** A student argues that bottom-up processing is more accurate than top-down processing because it avoids the influence of expectation. Which response best corrects this claim?
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-20 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Bottom-up processing is always more accurate, so the student is correct
- B. Neither type is more accurate in general; each has strengths, and perception usually combines them
- C. Top-down processing is always accurate because it uses stored knowledge
- D. Expectation has no effect on perception, so accuracy depends only on the stimulus

**Q673.** Which order of events best matches a bottom-up account of recognising an unfamiliar shape?
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-21 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. recognition of the object, then detection of its edges, then a guess about its context
- B. an expected object, then sensory input that confirms the expectation, then recognition
- C. a memory search, then the sensory input being ignored, then recognition
- D. detection of edges and features, then combining them into a shape, then recognition as an object

**Q674.** Why might people from different cultures perceive the same scene differently?
<!-- id: psych12a2-td-22 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Prior experience and cultural expectations shape how the same sensory input is interpreted
- B. People from different cultures have different wavelength sensitivities in their eyes
- C. Culture changes the number of photoreceptors each person has
- D. Some cultures lack bottom-up processing, so they rely only on expectation

#### Distortions of perception and illusions

*Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments) · Prerequisites: Depth cues and Gestalt principles*

**Q752.** In the Müller-Lyer illusion, two lines of equal length appear unequal because of
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-1 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. movement of the lines
- B. arrowheads or fins at their ends pointing inwards or outwards
- C. differences in colour
- D. different background brightness

**Q753.** In the Ames room, a person standing in one corner looks much larger than a person standing in the other corner. This occurs because
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-2 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the room is much larger than it appears
- B. the two people differ greatly in height
- C. the eyes have different focal lengths
- D. the viewer assumes the room is rectangular when it is actually distorted

**Q754.** Two identical tigers are drawn on a photo of a railway track, one near the bottom of the image and one near the top where the rails appear to converge. The upper tiger looks larger. This is the
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-3 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Ames room
- B. Ponzo illusion
- C. Müller-Lyer illusion
- D. Rubin’s vase

**Q755.** A perceptual illusion differs from a hallucination because an illusion
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-4 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. occurs without any stimulus
- B. is a misperception of a real stimulus
- C. only occurs in people with a mental disorder
- D. involves the sense of smell

**Q756.** Which is the most appropriate description of what a distortion of perception, such as the moon illusion, tells psychologists?
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-5 -->
*Info: Evaluation · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Sensory receptors are faulty in most people
- B. Illusions only occur in vision
- C. Perception is always an exact copy of the stimulus
- D. Perception is a constructive process in which the brain applies assumptions and cues to interpret sensory input

**Q757.** Explain, with reference to depth cues, why the moon looks larger when it is near the horizon than when it is high in the sky. (3 marks)
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-6 -->

**Q759.** Outline what is meant by a visual perceptual distortion and give one example other than the Müller-Lyer illusion. (2 marks)

**Q760.** Two parallel vertical lines drawn over a pattern of radiating lines appear to bow outwards. This is the Hering illusion, which shows that
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-9 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. a background pattern can distort the perceived straightness and orientation of nearby lines
- B. the two lines are physically bent by the radiating lines, which pull on them
- C. the eyes cannot focus on two lines that are placed close together
- D. lines that are physically straight can only look straight when they are horizontal

**Q761.** The Penrose triangle is a drawing that looks like a solid three-dimensional object but cannot exist as one. This is because
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-10 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the drawing has been printed with an error in its angles
- B. each corner looks plausible on its own, but the depth cues across the whole figure cannot be combined into one consistent object
- C. the viewer’s eyes cannot converge on a flat drawing
- D. the lines are too thin to be perceived as three-dimensional

**Q762.** A wire-frame cube drawn without shading can be seen in two different three-dimensional orientations, and the two views alternate. This is an example of
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-11 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. a size-constancy illusion
- B. a linear perspective depth cue
- C. an ambiguous figure, which has more than one stable interpretation
- D. a hallucination caused by fatigue

**Q763.** The same mid-grey square looks lighter when placed on a black background than when it is placed on a white background. This is best explained by
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-12 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the grey square reflects more light when placed on black
- B. a motion aftereffect caused by staring at the background
- C. the Müller-Lyer effect, in which fins at the ends of lines change apparent length
- D. simultaneous brightness contrast, in which the surrounding area changes the perceived lightness of a region

**Q764.** In the Zöllner illusion, long lines that are actually parallel look slanted or non-parallel because
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-13 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. short diagonal lines crossing the long lines distort the perceived angle of the long lines
- B. the long lines are slightly curved on the printed page
- C. parallel lines only look parallel when they are seen in peripheral vision
- D. the long lines are printed with different thicknesses

**Q765.** A row of identical circles is shaded darker at the bottom, so they look like bulges. When the same circles are shaded darker at the top, they look like hollows. This effect is due to
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-14 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. linear perspective, in which parallel lines converge with distance
- B. light and shadow, which the brain reads as showing the direction of light and the shape of a surface
- C. interposition, in which one object partly hides another
- D. aerial perspective, in which distant objects look hazy

**Q766.** A horizontal line and a vertical line are drawn with exactly the same length, yet the vertical line looks longer. This is the horizontal-vertical illusion, which is best explained by
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-15 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the vertical line being physically thicker than the horizontal line
- B. the horizontal line being partly hidden by a background pattern
- C. a tendency to overestimate vertical length relative to horizontal length
- D. the eyes scanning vertical lines more slowly, which makes them look stretched

**Q767.** A listener watches a video of a speaker’s lips making the sound ‘ga’ while hearing the sound ‘ba’, and often reports hearing ‘da’. This is the McGurk effect, which shows that
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-16 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. hearing is unaffected by what a person can see of the speaker
- B. visual information from the lips can change the sound that is perceived, so speech perception combines the senses
- C. the ears and eyes process speech in entirely separate, unconnected areas of the brain
- D. people who lip-read are always more accurate than people who only listen

**Q768.** Two boxes have exactly the same mass, but one is much larger than the other. When lifted, the smaller box feels heavier. This is the size-weight illusion, which is best explained by
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-17 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the smaller box being denser, so it has more mass
- B. the larger box being lifted more slowly, which reduces its perceived weight
- C. the brain expecting a larger object to be heavier, so the larger box feels lighter than expected
- D. the muscles in the hand tiring more quickly when lifting the larger box

**Q769.** In a completely dark room, a single small light that is actually stationary is viewed and appears to drift. The best explanation is
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-18 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the light physically moves because of air currents in the room
- B. the observer’s photoreceptors are fatigued by the bright light
- C. the observer is hallucinating because of the darkness
- D. the lack of reference points and small involuntary eye movements make a stationary light appear to move

**Q770.** Two lights placed a short distance apart are switched on one after the other in quick succession. The observer sees a single light moving from one position to the other. This is the phi phenomenon, which is an example of
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-19 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. apparent motion, in which a sequence of stationary stimuli is perceived as movement
- B. a motion aftereffect, caused by prolonged watching of movement
- C. real movement of light across the gap, caused by the lights’ brightness
- D. a depth cue, in which nearer lights appear to move faster

**Q771.** A sequence of tones is heard that seems to rise in pitch forever, yet the pattern repeats after a set interval. This auditory illusion is called
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-20 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the McGurk effect
- B. the Shepard tone, a paradox created by overlapping octaves with changing loudness
- C. the phi phenomenon
- D. the cocktail party effect, in which attention filters out background speech

**Q772.** A diagonal line passes behind a rectangle and emerges on the other side looking offset, even though the two visible parts lie on one straight line. This is the Poggendorff illusion, which is best explained by
<!-- id: psych12a3-dpi-21 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the line being physically broken into two pieces with a gap behind the rectangle
- B. the eyes converging unequally on the two parts of the line
- C. the rectangle’s vertical edges distorting the perceived angle of the diagonal, so the two parts look misaligned
- D. the background being darker on one side of the rectangle than the other

**Q781.** The Müller-Lyer lines still look different even after you measure them and know they are equal. This suggests that:
<!-- id: vce-psych12-di-c1 -->
*Challenge question*
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); multiple choice, as in school exams; harder than most exam questions*

- A. The lines really are different lengths, and the ruler used to measure them was inaccurate

### Research methods

**Q782.** A researcher investigates whether different amounts of sleep affect reaction time. The amount of sleep is the
<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-1 -->

- B. extraneous variable
- C. dependent variable
- D. independent variable

- A. Sleep affects people
- B. Participants who sleep for 8 hours will have faster reaction times than participants who sleep for 4 hours

<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-3 -->

- A. case study
- B. classification study

- D. correlational study

<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-4 -->

<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-5 -->

<!-- id: psych12a3-smv-14 -->

<!-- id: vce-psych12-sm-c1 -->
*Challenge question*
*Info: Investigation design · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams; harder than most exam questions*

- A. The sample was too large, so small differences in mood appear to be significant
- B. Mood cannot be measured, so the results cannot support any conclusion at all
- C. Correlations are always close to zero, so the link found must be due to chance
- D. It is correlational: nothing was manipulated, and other variables or reverse causation could explain the link

#### Ethics in psychological research

*Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on) · Prerequisites: Scientific methodologies and variables*

**Q877.** Informed consent in research means that participants
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-1 -->
*Info: Investigation design · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. are not told the aim at any stage
- B. are paid for taking part
- C. are told about the nature of the study, procedures and risks and agree to take part voluntarily
- D. can never withdraw

**Q878.** A researcher keeps participants’ names separate from their data and ensures that only the research team can access the data. This protects
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-2 -->
*Info: Data analysis · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. confidentiality
- B. deception
- C. voluntary participation
- D. debriefing

**Q879.** A study involves deceiving participants about its true aim. The most appropriate ethical safeguard to follow the experiment is
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-3 -->

- A. paying the participants
- B. a debriefing session
- C. destroying the data
- D. removing the control group

**Q880.** Which of the following best represents the principle of voluntary participation?
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-4 -->
*Info: Investigation design · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Participants are told they must complete the study to receive course credit

- C. Participants are offered a gift for staying
- D. Participants are told they cannot leave once they begin

- A. respect, responsibility and review

- C. replacement, reduction and refinement
- D. reliability, replication and reporting

<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-9 -->

- A. confidentiality
- B. the 3Rs

<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-11 -->

- A. stopping the task and offering support if a participant becomes very distressed
- B. increasing the difficulty of the task so that the results are more dramatic
- C. keeping the frustration hidden so that participants respond more naturally
- D. continuing the task because the participant consented at the start

<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-12 -->
*Info: Investigation design · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the student’s own tutor only
- B. the journal in which the results will be submitted
- C. a Human Research Ethics Committee
- D. the participants’ employers

**Q889.** Which of the following is an example of research misconduct?
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-13 -->
*Info: Investigation design · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. reporting a result that was not statistically significant
- B. sharing preliminary results at a conference before journal publication
- C. using a pilot study to refine procedures before the main study
- D. presenting another researcher’s ideas as your own without citing them

- A. research with high risk is acceptable as long as the findings are eventually published

- C. benefits only count if they increase the researcher’s career prospects
- D. a study should proceed only if its potential benefits outweigh the risks to participants

- A. adults with cognitive impairments who may be unable to give fully informed consent

- A. the researcher’s private bank account details

- D. a list of the names of other participants

**Q893.** A teacher invites students in their class to take part in a university researcher’s survey during class time, in front of their peers. What is the main ethical concern?

- A. the study will have too many participants to analyse

- C. the students may already know the researcher, which biases the results
- D. the study cannot be published in a school journal

**Q894.** A researcher changes the procedures of an approved study so that participants now complete a much longer task. What should the researcher do?
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-18 -->

- A. continue without telling anyone, because the original approval still applies
- B. inform only the journal that will publish the results
- C. destroy all data collected under the original procedures
- D. seek ethics approval for the changes and inform participants so they can decide whether to continue

**Q895.** During an interview, a participant discloses that they are at risk of harming themselves. What is the most ethical response?
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-19 -->
*Info: Investigation design · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. provide support and a referral to appropriate services, recognising that confidentiality has limits when a person is at risk
- B. keep it confidential and continue the interview as normal
- C. publish the disclosure in the findings to highlight the issue
- D. withdraw the participant’s data and end the interview without comment

**Q896.** A researcher publishes a direct quote from a participant, with enough workplace detail that colleagues could identify them, and does not ask for permission. Which ethical principle is breached?
<!-- id: psych12a3-eth-20 -->

- A. validity
- B. reliability

- A. to guarantee that the main study will produce significant results
- B. to identify problems that could harm or distress participants before the main study begins
- C. to avoid needing ethics approval for the main study
- D. to replace the need for informed consent in the main study

- A. Confidentiality, so that no one outside the study learns about the deception

#### Validity, reliability and errors

*Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on) · Prerequisites: Sampling and experimental designs*

**Q911.** A study’s results are said to be valid if
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-1 -->
*Info: Data analysis · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. the same results are obtained every time
- B. the study measures what it claims to measure and the conclusions drawn are justified by the data
- C. the sample is large
- D. the participants were happy to take part

**Q912.** In a study of caffeine and reaction time, the experimenter knows which participants received caffeine and unintentionally encourages them more. This is an example of
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-2 -->
*Info: Investigation design · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. a placebo effect
- B. an investigator effect
- C. random error
- D. a within-subjects design

**Q913.** Participants in a control group are given a sugar pill but believe it is an active drug and report feeling better. This is the
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-3 -->

- A. systematic error
- B. investigator effect
- C. placebo effect
- D. order effect

**Q914.** A stopwatch used in an experiment runs 0.5 seconds fast on every measurement. This is an example of
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-4 -->
*Info: Investigation design · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. systematic error

- D. random error

<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-9 -->

<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-10 -->

- A. internal validity
- B. inter-rater reliability
- C. external validity

**Q921.** A test is designed to measure intelligence. It gives the same score each time a person sits it, but it actually measures how quickly people can read words. The test is
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-11 -->
*Info: Investigation design · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. valid and reliable
- B. valid but not reliable
- C. reliable but not valid
- D. neither valid nor reliable

**Q922.** Participants in a memory study work out what the researcher hopes to find and try harder to produce the expected result. This is an example of
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-12 -->
*Info: Calculation · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. demand characteristics
- B. a double-blind procedure
- C. random error
- D. external validity

**Q923.** A reaction-time test is repeated on one participant five times, giving 241, 258, 236, 262 and 249 ms with no consistent pattern. The variation is best described as
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-13 -->
*Info: Investigation design · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. systematic error affecting accuracy
- B. random error affecting precision
- C. an outlier caused by the investigator
- D. a placebo effect

**Q924.** Which change is most likely to increase the reliability of a 10-item quiz that measures knowledge of one unit?
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-14 -->

- A. Making the questions much easier so that most students score highly
- B. Giving the quiz only once to a larger group of students
- C. Adding more questions that assess the same unit content
- D. Telling students that the quiz results will count towards their final grade

**Q925.** A researcher tests the effect of background music on maths performance using students working on computers in a quiet laboratory. The researcher then claims the results apply to students studying at home. The main concern is
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-15 -->
*Info: Data analysis · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. internal validity
- B. inter-rater reliability
- C. random error
- D. external validity

**Q926.** A set of kitchen scales always reads 2 g heavier than the true mass of any object placed on it. Which statement is correct?
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-16 -->
*Info: Investigation design · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. The scales have a systematic error, so their measurements are inaccurate
- B. The scales have random error, so their measurements are imprecise
- C. The scales are valid because their readings are perfectly consistent
- D. Taking repeated measurements and averaging them will remove the 2 g offset

**Q927.** Which of the following best describes an outlier in a set of data?
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-17 -->

- A. A score that lies in the same direction as most other scores
- B. A score that lies far from the rest of the data set
- C. The mean of all the scores in the data set

**Q928.** All participants complete Task A and then Task B. Their performance on Task B is better, but the researcher cannot tell whether this is due to the tasks or to practice gained on Task A. This problem is called
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-18 -->
*Info: Investigation design · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. a placebo effect
- B. random error
- C. an order effect
- D. a demand characteristic

**Q929.** A trial compares a new teaching program with a standard one. Which procedure best reduces investigator effect when students rate the programs?
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-19 -->

- A. Telling students in advance that the new program is expected to work
- B. Having the teacher who delivers each program also collect the ratings

- D. Using an independent assessor who does not know which group each student is in to collect the ratings

- B. The sample was too small to represent all students

- D. The results were not published in a peer-reviewed journal

**Q931.** Which statement about reliability and validity is correct?
<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-21 -->
*Info: Investigation design · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); multiple choice, as in school exams*

- A. Validity is necessary for reliability, but a valid measure need not be consistent
- B. Reliability and validity are the same concept described in different terms
- C. A valid measure must also be reliable, but a reliable measure may still be invalid
- D. A measure can be valid whenever the sample is large, even when its results vary widely

<!-- id: psych12a3-vre-23 -->

### Exam-style questions (mixed topics)

**Q956.** A skateboarding accident and the adolescent brain
<!-- id: vce-psych12-x1 -->
*Info: Conceptual, Investigation design · 10 marks · about 13 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); extended response, as in school exams and SACs · Topics: Structure and function of the brain, Acquired brain injury and neurological disorders, Neuroplasticity and adolescent brain development, Scientific methodologies and variables, Ethics in psychological research*
*Multi-part question · 10 marks · Original question in the style of a Units 1 & 2 end-of-semester exam*

Jordan, aged 16, fell from his skateboard without a helmet and injured the front of his brain (his frontal lobe).

**a.** Describe two functions of the frontal lobe that could be affected by Jordan's injury. *(2 marks)*

**b.** Explain why Jordan's injury is classified as an acquired brain injury. *(1 mark)*

**c.** Explain how neuroplasticity could help Jordan recover some of his abilities. *(2 marks)*

**d.** Jordan's friends often take risks on their skateboards. Explain why risk-taking is common in adolescence, referring to brain development. *(2 marks)*

A researcher compares the reaction times of 20 adolescents who have recently had a concussion with those of 20 adolescents who have not, using a computer task.

**e.** Identify the type of investigation and justify your answer. *(1 mark)*

**f.** Identify one ethical guideline the researcher must follow, and explain how it applies to this study. *(2 marks)*

**Q957.** First day at a new school
<!-- id: vce-psych12-x2 -->
*Info: Application, Conceptual · 10 marks · about 13 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); extended response, as in school exams and SACs · Topics: Person perception and attributions, Stereotypes, prejudice and discrimination, Conformity and group influence, Top-down and bottom-up processing, Heuristics and cognitive biases*
*Multi-part question · 10 marks · Original question in the style of a Units 1 & 2 end-of-semester exam*

Sam is starting at a new school. In the first class, Sam sees another student, Alex, arrive ten minutes late.

**a.** Sam decides that Alex is lazy. State whether this is a dispositional or a situational attribution, and name the error Sam may be making. *(2 marks)*

**b.** Explain how a stereotype could affect Sam's first impressions of other students. *(2 marks)*

**c.** At lunch, everyone in Sam's new friendship group wears their jumper tied around their waist, and Sam starts doing the same. Identify the type of social influence, and one factor in this situation that increases it. *(2 marks)*

**d.** Sam reads a canteen sign with a smudged letter: 'Pl_ase return your trays'. Explain how top-down processing helps Sam read it. *(2 marks)*

**e.** After hearing a friend's vivid story about getting lost in the school's buildings, Sam thinks it is very likely that Sam will get lost too. Name the heuristic Sam is using, and explain one risk of using it. *(2 marks)*

**Q958.** Attachment, thinking and identity
<!-- id: vce-psych12-x3 -->
*Info: Conceptual, Recall, Application · 10 marks · about 13 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); extended response, as in school exams and SACs · Topics: Emotional development and attachment, Cognitive development and Piaget, Psychosocial development and Erikson, Nature, nurture and the biopsychosocial approach, Typical and atypical behaviour*
*Multi-part question · 10 marks · Original question in the style of a Units 1 & 2 end-of-semester exam*

In Harlow's experiments, infant rhesus monkeys were raised with two artificial 'mothers': one made of wire that held a feeding bottle, and one covered in soft cloth that gave no food.

**a.** Describe the main finding of Harlow's experiments and what it suggests about attachment. *(2 marks)*

**b.** Identify one ethical concern with Harlow's experiments. *(1 mark)*

**c.** A 4-year-old says a tall, thin glass holds more juice than a short, wide glass, even after watching the same amount poured into each. Identify Piaget's stage and the concept the child lacks. *(2 marks)*

**d.** Describe Erikson's psychosocial stage for adolescence. *(2 marks)*

**e.** Use the biopsychosocial approach to explain a child's shyness. *(2 marks)*

**f.** Name one approach to deciding whether a behaviour is typical or atypical. *(1 mark)*

**Q959.** Seeing and tasting
<!-- id: vce-psych12-x4 -->
*Info: Conceptual, Recall · 10 marks · about 13 min · Exam relevance: Medium (core content for school assessments); extended response, as in school exams and SACs · Topics: Sensation and the visual system, Distortions of perception and illusions, Depth cues and Gestalt principles, Gustatory perception*
*Multi-part question · 10 marks · Original question in the style of a Units 1 & 2 end-of-semester exam*

Visual perception begins when light enters the eye.

**a.** Describe the processes of reception, transduction and transmission in the visual system. *(3 marks)*

**b.** In the Müller-Lyer illusion, two equal lines look different in length. Give one explanation. *(2 marks)*

**c.** Explain retinal disparity as a depth cue. *(2 marks)*

**d.** Define the Gestalt principle of closure. *(1 mark)*

**e.** Explain why food tastes bland when you have a blocked nose. *(2 marks)*

**Q960.** Challenge: a risky decision at sixteen
<!-- id: vce-psych12-cx1 -->
*Challenge question*
*Info: Conceptual, Application · 10 marks · about 13 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); extended response, as in school exams and SACs; harder than most exam questions · Topics: Neuroplasticity and adolescent brain development, Nature, nurture and the biopsychosocial approach, Conformity and group influence, Heuristics and cognitive biases*
*Multi-part question · 10 marks · Original challenge question in the style of a Units 1 & 2 end-of-semester exam*

Jess, 16, is at a party where her friends are jumping from a roof into a pool. She feels anxious but jumps anyway. Later she says, 'I knew it was dangerous, but everyone else was doing it and I didn't think I would get hurt.' Jess's older brother had a similar accident last year.

**a.** Explain how the development of Jess's brain could have contributed to her decision. *(2 marks)*

**b.** Identify one biological, one psychological and one social factor in this scenario. *(3 marks)*

**c.** Explain Jess's behaviour in terms of normative influence. *(2 marks)*

**d.** Jess knew her brother had been hurt, but still thought she wouldn't be. Name and explain this bias. *(2 marks)*

**e.** Explain why adolescence is described as a period of significant neuroplasticity. *(1 mark)*

**Q961.** Challenge: does context change what we see?
<!-- id: vce-psych12-cx2 -->
*Challenge question*
*Info: Investigation design, Calculation, Conceptual, Data analysis · 10 marks · about 13 min · Exam relevance: High (core content that Units 3 & 4 build on); extended response, as in school exams and SACs; harder than most exam questions · Topics: Scientific methodologies and variables, Top-down and bottom-up processing, Sampling and experimental designs, Sensation and the visual system, Data analysis and conclusions*
*Multi-part question · 10 marks · Original challenge question in the style of a Units 1 & 2 end-of-semester exam*

Forty Year 11 students were randomly allocated to two groups. Group 1 saw an ambiguous figure that could be read as 'B' or '13', placed in the sequence A ? C. Group 2 saw the same figure in the sequence 12 ? 14. Each participant wrote what they saw.

| Group | Reported 'B' | Reported '13' |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1: letters (A ? C) | 17 | 3 |
| 2: numbers (12 ? 14) | 4 | 16 |

**a.** Identify the independent and dependent variables. *(2 marks)*

**b.** Calculate the percentage of each group that reported the response matching its context, and explain the results. *(3 marks)*

**c.** Name the experimental design and state one limitation of it here. *(2 marks)*

**d.** Distinguish between sensation and perception in this task. *(2 marks)*

**e.** Give one reason the conclusion may not generalise to all people. *(1 mark)*

#### Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander approaches to learning

*Exam relevance: Medium (comes up regularly in exams) · Prerequisites: Observational learning*

<!-- id: psych34a2-atsi-1 -->

- A. songlines
- B. the method of loci applied to a written map

- A. Learning is individual and competitive, with a focus on personal achievement
- B. Learning is delivered only by written texts

- A. They link information to meaningful, emotional, multisensory experiences that are shared and repeated, which supports memory

<!-- id: psych34a2-atsi-5 -->

- A. are separate from cultural knowledge
- B. decide who is allowed to attend school
- C. define relationships and responsibilities, including who teaches particular knowledge and to whom
- D. replace the need for oral traditions

### Unit 3 AOS 2: Memory

#### Atkinson-Shiffrin multi-store model

*Exam relevance: High (comes up in most exams) · Prerequisites: none*

**Q1070.** In the Atkinson-Shiffrin multi-store model of memory, which of the following is the correct order in which information flows when it is successfully retained for a long time?
<!-- id: psych34a2-asm-1 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (comes up in most exams); multiple choice, as in Section A*

- A. Sensory memory, long-term memory, short-term memory
- B. Long-term memory, short-term memory, sensory memory
- C. Short-term memory, sensory memory, long-term memory
- D. Sensory memory, short-term memory, long-term memory

**Q1071.** Which of the following best describes the capacity and duration of short-term memory according to the Atkinson-Shiffrin model?
<!-- id: psych34a2-asm-2 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (comes up in most exams); multiple choice, as in Section A*

- A. Unlimited capacity and potentially permanent duration
- B. About 7 ± 2 items and about 12 to 30 seconds without rehearsal
- C. About 7 ± 2 items and several minutes without rehearsal
- D. A few hundred items and about 3 to 4 seconds

**Q1072.** Brian looks at a street scene as he walks past. He can recall almost nothing about it a minute later, because he did not pay attention to most of it. This is best explained by
<!-- id: psych34a2-asm-3 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (comes up in most exams); multiple choice, as in Section A*

- A. a lack of retrieval cues in long-term memory
- B. failure of procedural memory
- C. information being displaced from long-term memory
- D. information in sensory memory decaying because it was not attended to

**Q1073.** A researcher presents participants with a list of 20 words and then tests recall. People commonly recall the first few and last few words best. The better recall of the first words is typically explained by the Atkinson-Shiffrin model as
<!-- id: psych34a2-asm-4 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (comes up in most exams); multiple choice, as in Section A*

- A. more rehearsal, so these words are more likely to be transferred to long-term memory
- B. the words being stored in procedural memory
- C. the last words displacing the first words from long-term memory
- D. the words still being held in sensory memory

**Q1074.** Which of the following is a limitation of the Atkinson-Shiffrin multi-store model?
<!-- id: psych34a2-asm-5 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (comes up in most exams); multiple choice, as in Section A*

- A. It suggests that memory has more than one store
- B. It describes sensory memory as having a very short duration
- C. It treats short-term memory as a single store and does not explain how different types of long-term memory work or the role of processing beyond rehearsal
- D. It proposes that information can be rehearsed

**Q1075.** Distinguish between iconic memory and echoic memory, including the typical duration of each. (3 marks)
<!-- id: psych34a2-asm-6 -->
*Info: Conceptual · about 3 marks · about 4 min · Exam relevance: High (comes up in most exams); short answer, as in Section B*

*Written answer*

**Q1076.** Maya is trying to remember a phone number she has just been told. Using the Atkinson-Shiffrin multi-store model, explain how she could ensure the number is stored for a long period of time, and what will most likely happen if she is interrupted straight after hearing it. (4 marks)
<!-- id: psych34a2-asm-7 -->
*Info: Application · about 4 marks · about 5 min · Exam relevance: High (comes up in most exams); short answer, as in Section B*

*Written answer*

**Q1077.** Describe how the serial position effect provides evidence for separate short-term and long-term memory stores. (3 marks)
<!-- id: psych34a2-asm-8 -->
*Info: Conceptual · about 3 marks · about 4 min · Exam relevance: High (comes up in most exams); short answer, as in Section B*

*Written answer*

**Q1078.** You were not listening, but a second after a friend asks a question you can 'replay' it and answer. Which memory store made this possible?
<!-- id: vce-psych34-as-c1 -->
*Challenge question*
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (comes up in most exams); multiple choice, as in Section A; harder than most exam questions*

- A. Iconic sensory memory, which holds images for about 0.2–0.4 seconds
- B. Long-term memory, because it has unlimited capacity
- C. Echoic sensory memory, which holds sounds for about 3–4 seconds
- D. Short-term memory, because it lasts 18–30 seconds without attention

*Exam relevance: High (comes up in most exams) · Prerequisites: Atkinson-Shiffrin multi-store model*

**Q1079.** Which of the following is an example of implicit memory?

- A. Recalling the date of your last birthday party

<!-- id: psych34a2-eim-2 -->

- B. memory of general facts and knowledge about the world
- C. memory of skills and habits
- D. memory of conditioned emotional responses

**Q1081.** A participant is shown a series of words and later asked to complete word fragments such as 'ch_ir'. They are more likely to complete fragments with previously seen words, but do not remember seeing the list. This is evidence of
<!-- id: psych34a2-eim-3 -->
*Info: Recall · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (comes up in most exams); multiple choice, as in Section A*

- A. implicit memory, because past experience influenced performance without conscious recollection

- C. the serial position effect

- B. Semantic memory: recalling what you ate for breakfast yesterday

**Q1083.** Which statement correctly distinguishes explicit memory from implicit memory?

#### Circadian rhythm sleep disorders and treatments

*Exam relevance: High (comes up in most exams) · Prerequisites: Circadian and ultradian rhythms*

**Q1169.** Delayed sleep phase disorder (DSPD) is characterised by
<!-- id: psych34a3-crsd-1 -->

- A. falling asleep and waking much earlier than desired

- C. sleep that is normal in length but frequently interrupted by awakenings
- D. excessive daytime sleepiness caused by narcotic drugs

- A. Systematic desensitisation
- B. Sleep restriction with caffeine
- C. Benzodiazepine therapy
- D. Bright light therapy

- B. Her homeostatic sleep drive has permanently stopped
- C. She has developed advanced sleep phase disorder from taking melatonin

### Unit 4 AOS 2: Mental wellbeing

#### Mental wellbeing and the mental health continuum

*Exam relevance: High (comes up in most exams) · Prerequisites: Mental wellbeing and categorising mental disorders (Psychology, Units 1 & 2)*

**Q1187.** Which of the following best describes mental wellbeing?
<!-- id: psych34a3-mw-1 -->
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (comes up in most exams); multiple choice, as in Section A*

- A. A state in which a person can realise their abilities, cope with normal stressors, work productively and contribute to their community
- B. A permanent state that does not change over time
- C. The same thing as happiness
- D. The absence of any mental disorder

**Q1188.** On the mental health continuum, a person who is functioning well, has good coping and feels able to take part in life is placed
<!-- id: psych34a3-mw-2 -->
*Info: Application · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (comes up in most exams); multiple choice, as in Section A*

- A. in the middle, as a mental health problem
- B. at the mentally healthy / good mental wellbeing end
- C. at the mental illness end
- D. outside the continuum

**Q1189.** Which statement about the difference between a mental health problem and a mental disorder is most accurate?
<!-- id: psych34a3-mw-3 -->

- A. A mental health problem interferes with a person's thoughts, feelings or behaviour but is generally less severe and shorter lasting than a mental disorder, which meets diagnostic criteria and persists over time
- B. They are identical terms
- C. A mental disorder never involves a change in functioning
- D. A mental health problem is a diagnosable condition and a mental disorder is not

- B. flourishing at the top of the continuum

<!-- id: psych34a3-mw-5 -->

- A. People's mental health can vary in degree and shift position over time in response to internal and external factors
- B. Only people with mental illness change position
- C. Mental health is fixed throughout life

<!-- id: vce-psych34-pf-c1 -->
*Challenge question*
*Info: Conceptual · 1 mark · about 1 min · Exam relevance: High (comes up in most exams); multiple choice, as in Section A; harder than most exam questions*

- A. Operant conditioning: avoidance is negatively reinforced by the removal of anxiety
